UNIT 109 At/on/in (time)

b

d

e At We use at with times: at 5 oclock at 11:45 at midnight at lunchtime Carol usually leaves work at five o’clock. But we usually leave out at when we ask (At) what time...?: m What time are you going out this evening? We also use at in these expressions:

at night
at Christmas / at Easter (public holiday periods) at the moment / at present at the same time
at the age of... at the beginning of... at the end of... I don’t like going out at night. We give each other presents at Christmas.
Ms. King is busy at the moment / at present. Ann and I arrived at the same time. Tom left school at the age of 16/ at 16. I’m going away at the beginning of May. At the end of the concert, there was great applause.

On We use on with dates and days: on March 12th on Friday(s) on Christmas Day (but at Christmas) w They got married on March 12th. We also say:
on Friday morning(s) on Sunday afternoon(s) on Monday evening(S) on Saturday night(s), etc. on weekends m I usually go out on Monday evenings. What are you doing on the weekend?
In. We use in for longer periods of time (for example: months/years/seasons): in April in 1968 in (the) winter in the 18th century in the 1970s in the Middle Ages m They got married in 1968. We also say: in the morning(s) / in the afternoon(S) / in the evening(s) m Ill see you in the morning. (but T’ll see you on Friday morning.)
We do not use at/on/in before last and next: mIl see you next Friday. • They got married last March.
In + a period of time = a time in the future: The train will be leaving in a few minutes. (= a few minutes from now) a Jack went away. Hell be back in a week. (= a week from now) m They are getting married in six months. (= six months from now) You can also say “in six months time,” “in a week’s time,” etc.: m They are getting married in six months time. We also use in to say how long it takes to do something: wIlearned to drive in four weeks. (= it took me four weeks to learn)

UNIT 110 For, during, and while

For and during We use for + a period of time to say how long something goes on: for six years for two hours for a week
I’ve lived in this house for six years. We watched television for two hours last night. • Ann is going away for a week in September. w Where have you been? F’ve been waiting for hours. m Are you going away for the weekend? You cannot use during in this way: wIt rained for three days without stopping. (not during three days)
We use during + noun to say when something happens (not how long): during the movie during our vacation during the night • Ifell asleep during the movie.
w we met a lot of interesting people during our vacation. Fhcalound to mie hint us hare ated doning the mightt
b During and while We use during + noun. We use while + subject + verb. Compare:

NOUN
Ifell asleep during the movie.
subject + verb
Itell asleep While I was watching television.
Compare during and while in these examples: • We met a lot of interesting people during our vacation. We met a lot of interesting people while we were on vacation. • Robert suddenly began to feel sick during the exam. Robert suddenly began to feel sick while he was taking the exam. Here are some more examples of while:
m We saw Ann while we were waiting for the bus.
While you were out, there was a phone call for you. m Tom read a book while I watched television. When you are talking about the future, use the present (not will) after while: mI’m going to Toronto next week. I hope to see Tom while I’m there. w What are you going to do while you are waiting? See also Unit 9a.
For while-ing see Unit 64b. For for and since see Unit 19b.

UNIT
111 By and until
By the time ...

b By (+ atime)= not later than:
m I mailed the letter today, so they should receive it by Monday. (= on or before Monday, on Monday at the latest) w We’d better hurry. We have to be home by S o*clock (= at or before S O’clock, at S o clock at the latest) m Where’s Ann? She should be here by now. (= now or before now; so she should have already arrived)
You cannot use until with this meaning: w Tell me by Friday whether or not you can come to the party. (not Tell me until Friday) We use until (or till) to say how long a situation continues: m ‘Shall we go now?” “No, let’s wait until (or till) it stops raining.” I was tired this morning, so I stayed in bed until half past ten. Compare until and by in these sentences: m Sue will be away until Monday. (so shell come back on Monday) m Sue will be back by Monday. (= shell be back on or before Monday, on Monday at the latest) m Tl be working until 11 o’clock. (so T’l stop working at 11 o’clock) m Ill have finished my work by 11 o’clock (= Tll finish my work at or before 11 o’clock, at 11 o’clock at the latest)
You can also say by the time (something happens),•·.. Study these examples carefully: 뼈 It’s not worth going shopping now. By the time we get to the stores, they will be closed. (= they will close between now and the time we get there) m (from aleter) Im fying to the United States this evening. So by the time You receive this letter, Ill probably be in New York. (= iwil arrive in New York between now and the time you receive this letter.)
When you are talking about the past, you can use By the time (something happened),... m Tom’s car broke down on the way to the party last night. By the time he arrived, most of the guests had left. (= It took him a long time to get to the Party and most of the guests left during this time.) w Ihad a lot of work to do yesterday evening. By the time I finished, I was very tired. (= It took me a long time to do the work and I became more and more tired during this time.) m It took them a long time to find a place to park their car. By the time they got to the theater, the play had already started. You can also use by then or by that time: • Tom finally arrived at the party at midnight. But by then (or by that time), most of the guests had left.

UNIT 113 In/at/on (position) (2)

“ We say that someone is at an event. For example: “at a party /at a concert / at a conference / at the movies / at a football game : Were there many people at the party /at the meeting? I saw Jack at the football game / at the concert on Saturday. b

e we say:

at work

• Tom’s tather is in the hospital. You can say be home / stay home with or without at: m We’ll be out during the day but we™ll be (at) home all evening. mI didn’t go out last night. I stayed (at) home.
You can be in or at college/school. Use at college or at school when you are thinking of the college/school as a place or when you give the name of a college/school: a Dan will be in college / in school for two more years. m Tom is away at college right now, but he’ll be home for the summer. m She’s majoring in economics at Los Angeles City College.
You can often use in or at with buildings. You can stay in a hotel or at a hotel; you Can eat in a restaurant or at a restaurant. We usually say at when we say where an event takes place (for example: a concert, a movie, a meeting, a sports event, etc.): m We went to a concert at the Arts Center. m The meeting took place at the company’s main office. • ‘Where were you last night? “At the theater.” We say at someone’s house:
• I was at Tom’s house last night. (or I was at Tom’s last night.) We use in when we are thinking about the building itself: w The rooms in Tom’s house are very small. m Ienjoyed the movie, but it was very cold in the theater.
We usually say in with towns and villages: w Tom’s parents live in St. Louis. (not“at St. Louis”) But you can use at when the town or village is a point on a journey: • Do you know if this train stops at Smithtown? m We stopped at a pretty town on the way to Los Angeles.
We say arrive IN a country or town: m When did he arrive in Japan /in Tokyo? We say arrive AT with other places (buildings, etc.) or events: m What time did he arrive at school/ at work /at the hotel / at the party? We say arrive home (without a preposition): • When did he arrive home?

a

d To We say go/come/travel (etc.) to a place or event. For example:
go to Brazil come to the U.S. fly to Tokyo walk to work go to the bank 영 to a party be sent to prison be taken to the hospital return to Italy drive to the airport 80 10 acencent
We say get to (but arrive in/at - (see Unit 113f): m What time did you get to Montreal/ work / the party? We say go home/ come home/get home, etc. (with no preposition): T’m tired. Let’s go home. m What time did you get home last night?
Been to I have been to (a place)= I have visited a place; I went there, but now I have come back (see also Unit 13d): m Have you ever been to Japan? m I’ve been to Buenos Aires twice. m Ann has never been to a football game in her life. • Jack has plenty of money. He has just been to the bank.
Into “Go into / come into,” etc. = enter (a room / building, etc.): m l opened the door and went into the room. Don’t wait outside! Come into the house. INTO
a The man the police were chasing ran into a store. • A bird few into the room through the window.
By car / in my car We use by ...to say how we travel:
by car
also: by train by plane by boat/ship by bus by bicycle by rail by air by sea by subway
m “How did you go to Paris?’ “By plane.” w Sue usually goes to work by bicycle/ by car / by bus / by train. But we say “on foot”:
m Did you come here by car or on foot? But you cannot use by if you say my car/ the train / a taxi,” etc. We say in my car” (nor by my car), “on the train” (not by the train).
We use in for cars and taxis:
in my car in Tom’s car in the car in a car in a taxi We say get in(to) / get out of a car or taxi: He got into the car and drove off. (or He got in the car...)
We use on for bicycles and public transportation (buses, trains, etc.): on my bicycle on the bus on the 6:45 train on a big ship We say get on / get off a bicycle, bus, or train : Quick! Get on the train. It’s ready to leave.

1 rise/ an increase/ a tall/ a decrease IN something a There has been an increase in automobile accidents lately

뼈 Nobody knows what the cause of the explosion was.

• Did you get an invitation to the party?
a reaction TO something:
m I was surprised at her reaction to what I said.

an attitude TO/TOWARD someone/something: m His attitude to/toward his job is very negative.
a relationship/ a connection / contact WITH someone/something: m Do you have a good relationship with your parents? m The police want to question a man in connection with the robbery. but: a relationship /a connection /a difference BETWEEN two things: m The police have said that there is no connection between the two murders.

UNIT 121 Verb + prepostion (3)
Study this list of verbs + preposition:

search (a person/a place /a bag, ctc.) FOR someone/something: m I’ve searched the whole house for my keys, but I still can’t find them. m The police are searching for the escaped prisoner.

m He was very angry and started shouting at me. bul: shout TO someone (so that they can hear you): m He shouted to me from the other side of the street.
speak/talk TO someone (‘with” is also possible): m (on the telephone) Hello, can I speak to Jane, please? m Who was that man I saw you talking to in the restaurant?
Suffer FROM an illness:
m The number of people suffering from heart disease has increased.
think ABOUT someone/something (= consider, concentrate the mind on): w You’re quict this morning. What are you thinking about? m Ive thought about what you said and Ive decided to take your advice. m”Will you lend me the money?” “Tll think about it.” think Or someone/something (= remember, bring to mind, have an idea): m She told me her name, but I can’t think of it now. (not think about it) m That’s a good idea. Why didn’t I think of that? We also use think OF when we ask for or give an opinion: m ‘What did you think of the movie?” “T didn t think much ofit.” The difference between think OF and think ABOUT is sometimes very small. Often you can use OF Or ABOUT:
m My sister is thinking of (or about) going to Canada.
m lom was thinking of (or about) buying a new car, but changed his mind. m When T’m alone, I often think of (or about) you.
wait FOR someone/something: m T’m not going out yet. I’m waiting for the rain to stop.
write TO someone or write someone (without to): 뼈 Sorry I haven’t written (to) you for such a long time. m I wrote her a letter.

We do not use a preposition with these verbs:
call/phone someone Did you call/phone your father yesterday? discuss something We diseussed many things at the meeting. enter (= go into a place) She felt nervous as she entered the room.
For verb + preposition +-ing see Unit 57a.

UNIT 122 Verb + object + preposition (1)

Study this list of verbs + object + preposition:

accuse someone OF (doing) something (see also Unit 57b): m Tom accused Ann of being selfish m Three students were accused of cheating on the exam.
ask (someone) FOR something: mI wrote to the company asking them for more information about the job. but: “ask (someone) a question” (no preposition)
blame someone/something FOR something: m Everybody blamed me for the accident. or: blame something ON someone/something: m Everybody blamed the accident on me. We also say:“(someone is) to blame for something”:
m Everybody said that I was to blame for the accident.
borrow something FROM someone:
mI didn’t have any money. I had to borrow some from a friend of mine.
charge someone WITH (an offense / a crime): m Three men have been arrested and charged with robbery.
ongratulate someone ON (doing) something (see also Unit 57b): a When I heard that she had passed her exams, I called her t
congratulate her on her Success.
divide/cut/split something INTO (two or more parts): w The book is divided into three parts. m Cut the meat into small pieces before frying it.
do something ABOUT something (= do something to improve a bad situation): m The economic situation is getting worse and worse. The government ought to do something about it.
explain (a problem / a situation/a word, etc.) TO someone: m Can you explain this word to me? (not explain me this word) also: “explain (to someone) that/what/how/why ...” (note the word order): m Let me explain to you what I mean.
invite someone TO (a party / a wedding, etc.): m Have you been invited to any parties recently?
leave (a place) FOR (another place): mIhaven’t seen her since she left home for work this morning.
point/aim something AT someone/something: m Don’t point that knife at me! It’s dangerous.

UNIT 124 Phrasal verbs (get up, break down, fill in, etc.)

a We often use verbs with these words:

back ofi in Out
over about around forward
We often use these words with verbs of movement. For example: down through

get on
drive oft
come back
turn around
But often these words (on/off/up/down, etc.) give a special meaning to a verb. For example: m Sorry I’m late. The car broke down. • Look out! There’s a car coming. It was my first flight. I was very nervous as the plane took off. 1I was so tired this morning that I couldn’t get up. These verbs (break down / get up / take oft, etc.) are phrasal verbs.
Sometimes a phrasal verb has an object. Usually there are two possible positions for the object. So you can say:
object
I turned off the light. or object
I turned the light off. Here are some more examples:
Imill out this form? m Could you I mill this form out?

m It’s warm. Take oft your coat. Take your coat off.
The fire fighters soon arrived and , Put out the fire put the fire ou

※ I think TIl Ithrow away these old newspapers. Ithrow these old newspapers away.
) breaking down the door a The police got into the house by 1 breaking the door down
Sometimes the object of a phrasal verb is a pronoun (it/them/me/you/him/her/us). These pronouns go before on/off/in/out/up/down, etc.: m They gave me a form and told me to fill it out. (not fill out it) m Ann’s asleep. Don’t wake her up. (not wake up her) m What should I do with these old newspapers?” “Throw them away.” • Here’s the money you need. Don’t forget to pay me back.
Sometimes we use a phrasal verb + preposition. For example: look forward to / keep up with / cut down on. The object always comes afier the preposition: m Are you looking forward to your vacation? m You’re walking too fast. Ican’t keep up with you. a Jack has cut down on smoking. He only smokes five cigarettes a day now.

Present perfect Ihave done (Units 13-15, 17-20) Ive lost my key. Have you seen it anywhere? “Is Tom here?” “No, he has gone home.”
How long have they been married? The house is very dirty. We haven’t cleaned it for weeks.
Present perfect contintous I have been doing (Units 16-18)

I haven’t been feeling very well lately.

What time did you get up this morning? It was hot in the room, so she opened the window.

APPENDIX 2 Regular and irregular verbs

1. Regular verbs
The simple past and past participle of regular verbs end in-ed. For example:
base form: simple pastpast participle: Clean
cleaned
For spelling rules see Appendix 3. r mprove paint bainted caficed

For the simple past see Units 11 and 20. We use the past participle to make the perfect tenses (have/has/had cleaned) and for all the passive forms (see Units 40~42):
m I cleaned my room yesterday (simple past) Your English has improved. (present perfect- see Units 13-15, 17-20) The house was dirty. We hadn’t cleaned it for a long time. (past perfeet- see Unit 21) This door has just been painted. (present perfect passtve) m He was carried out of the room. (simple past passive)
2. Irregular verbs
With some irregular verbs, all thrce forms (base form, simple past, and past participle) are the same. For example, hit: m Someone hit me as Icame into the room. (simple past) T’ve never hit anyone in my life. (past participle - present perfect) m George was hit on the head by a rock. (past participle - passive)
With other irregular verbs, the simple past is the same as the past participle (but different from the base form ). For cxample, tell - told:
She told me to come back the next day. (simple past)
Have you told anyone about your new job? (past participle - present perfect) 뼈 I was told to come back the next day. (past parliciple - passive) With other irregular verbs all three forms are different. For example, break - broke - broken:
w He broke his arm in a climbing accident. (simple past) a Somebody has broken the window. (pas parliciple - present perfecr) m When was the window broken? (past parliciple- Passive)

APPENDIX 3 Spelling
Nouns, verbs, and adjectives can have the following endings:
noun +-s/es (plural) books ideas verb + -sles (after he/she/it) works enjoys verb + -ing working enjoying verb + -ed worked enjoyer adjective +-er (comparative) cheaper quicke adjective +-est (superlative) cheapest adjective +-ly (adverb) cheaply

When we use these endings, there are sometimes changes in spelling. These changes are listed below.
Vowels and consonants
a e i o uare vowel letters.
The other letters (b c d fetc.) are consorants.

1. Nouns and verbs +-s/-es
The ending is-es when the word ends in -s/-ss/-sh/-ch/-x: match/matches bus/buses box/boxes wash/washes miss/misses search/searches Nore also:
potato/potatoes do/does tomato/tomatoes
go/goes
2. Words ending in -y (baby, carry, easy, etc.)
If a word ends in a consonant +y (-by/-ry/-sy, etc.):
Y changes to ie before es:
baby/babies family/families hurry/hurries study/studies country/countries
apply/applies secretary/secretaries try/tries

study/studied apply/applied
y changes to i before -er and-est:
easy/easier/easiest heavy/heavier/heaviest
y changes to i before -ly: easy/easily heavg/heavily temporary/temporarily try/tried

lucky/luckier/luckiest

y does not change before-ing:
1uTrying studying applying trying
y does not change if the word ends in a vowel + y (-ay/-ey/-oy/-uy): play/plays/played enjoy/enjoys/enjoyed monkey/monkeys exception: day/daily Note also: pay/paid lay/laid Say/said
3. Verbs ending in -ie (die, lie, tie)
If a verb ends in ie, ie changes to y before-ing: lie/lying die/dying tie/tying

256

4. Words ending in e (smoke, hope, wide, etc.)
Verbs

t a verb ends 1n e, 1
smoke/smoking wp etopinut c hclice iring contise comiusing
Exceptions: be/being verbs ending in ee: see/seeing agree/agrecing

smoke/smoked hope/hoped
Adjectives and adverbs
If an adiective ends in e, we add-r and-st for the comparative and superlative
wide/wider/widest late/later/latest large/arger/largest

polite/politely extreme/extremely
If an adjective ends in le (terrible, probable, etc.), we leave out e and add -y for the adverb: terrible/terribly probable/probably reasonable/reasonably

5; Doubling consonants (stop/stopping/stopped, hot/hotter/hottest, etc.)
Sometimes a verb or an adiective ends in consonant - vowel - consonant. For example:
stop plan hot thin wet prefer begin
We double the final consonant (-pp-, nn- etc.) of these words before-ing, -ed,-er and -est: stop/stopping/stopped plan/planning/planned rob/robbing/robbed
hot/hotter/hottest thin/thinner/thinnest wct/wetter/wettest
If the word has more than one syllable (prefer, begin, etc.), we double that final consonant only if the final syllable is stressed: PreFER/preferring/prcferred perMIT/permitting/permitted
reGRET/regretting/regretted beGIN/beginning
If the final syllable is not stressed, we do mor double the final consonant:
VISit/visiting/visited de VELop/developing/developed LISten/listening/istened reMEMber/remembering/remembered
If the final syllable is not stressed, and the last consonant is I, the consonant
may be single or doubled: travel/traveling/traveled or travelling/travelled cancel/canceling/canceled or cancelling/cancelled
We do not double the final consonant if the word ends in two consonants (-rt, -rn, -ck, etc.): start/starting/started turn/turning/turned thick/thicker/thickest
We do not double the final consonant if there are two vowel letters before it (-oil, -eed, -ain, etc.):
boil/boiling/boiled need/needing/nceded explain/explaining/explained
cheap/cheaper/cheapest loud/louder/loudest quiet/quicter/quietest
Note that we do not double y or w at the end of words. (At the end of words y and w are not consonants; they are part of the vowel sound.): stay/staying/stayed grow/growing new/newer/newest

APPENDIX 4 Short forms (1’m/didn’t, etc.)
n spoken English we usually say Tm/you’ve/didn’t,” etc. (=I am/you have/did not). We also use these short torms In wjormal Written English (for example, in letters to friends). When we write short forms, We use an apostrophe (’) for the missing letter or letters: 1m = 1 am you’ve = you have didn’t = did not Short forms of auxiliary verbs (am/is/are/have/has/had/will/shall/would):
2m= am I’m ’s = is or has he’s she’s it’s Pre = are
‘ve = have I’ve 개 = will or shall ‘d = would or had I’d he lI he’d she !! she’d iCl you’re you’ve you’|! yourd we’re we’ve we !! werd they’re they’v

’s can be is or has: w He’s sick. (= He is sick.) He’s gone away. (= He has gone away.) ‘d can be would or had:
T’d sec a doctor ifT were you. (=Twould see) I’d never seen her before. (=Ihad never seen) We use some of these short forms after question words (who/what/how, etc.) and after that/there/here: who’s What’s Where’s that’s there’s who’ll What’|! when’s that’|! there’|! who’d how’s here’s

m Who’s that girl over there? (= who is) m What’s happened? (= what has) mI think there’l be a lot of people at the party. (= there will) Sometimes we use short forms (especially ’s) after a noun: m John’s going out tonight. (= John is going) w My friend’s just gotten married. (= My friend has just gotten) You cannot use these short forms (‘m/’s/’ve, etc.) at the end of a sentence (because the verb is stressed in this position):
m“Are you tired?” “Yes, I am.”(not“Yes, I’m.”) • Do you know where he is? (not Do you know where he’s?)
Short forms of auxiliary verbs + not (isn’t/didn’t, ctc.):
isn’t (= is not) haven’t (= have not) aren’t (= are not) hasn’t (= has not) wasn’t (= was not) hadn’t wouldn’t (= would not) shouldn’t (= should not) (= had not) weren’t (= were not) Can’t (= cannot) don’t (= do not) mustn’t (= must not) couldn’t (= could not) doesn’t (= does not) Won’t (= will not) didn’t (= did not)
Note that you can say: he isn’t/she isn’t/it isnt or he’s not/she’s not/it’s not you aren’t/we aren’t// or you’re not/we’re not/they’re not
